L1569: ## 19.2.2.1 Dose-Response Analyses for Efficacy
L1570: Multiple efficacy endpoints including ORR, PFS and OS were compared between Combo 300 and Combo 450. The results of confirmed ORR by BIRC were first compared. The confirmed ORR appears to be comparable between Combo 450 (63.0 (95% CI: 55.8, 69.9)) and Combo 300 (65.9 (95% CI: 59.8, 71.7)), and ORR in 2 monotherapy arms in part 1 (50.5%) and part2 (50.4%) are also comparable.
L1571: The Kaplan-Meier (KM) curve of PFS for monotherapy and combination therapy in each part of study CMEK162B2301 were presented in Figure 30. The median time to PFS is 15.5 months for Combo 450 and 9.56 months for Mono 300 in part 1, and 12.88 months for Combo 300 and 7.36 months for Mono 300 in part 2, respectively. The numerical increase in median PFS
L1572: ## Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 210496
## BRAFTOVI™ (encorafenib)
L1573: comparing combination therapy to its randomized monotherapy is comparable (5.94 months vs 5.52 months). The difference in time to PFS between different treatment groups were characterized by a Cox proportional-hazards (CPH) model. A full model approach was first applied to incorporate all the known risk factors. The covariates with p-value larger than 0.05 were removed from the full model after backward elimination. The risk factors included in the full model include: Age, Gender, Body weight, Race, Region, Primary tumor site, Metastatic disease, Cancer stage at baseline per IWRS, Brain Metastases, Baseline LDH, ECOG score, BRAF mutation status and Number of organs.
L1574: The HRs obtained from final cox regression characterizing direct comparison of time to PFS of Combo 450 versus Mono 300 in part 1, Combo 300 versus Mono 300 in part 2 and cross-part comparison of Combo 450 versus Combo 300 and Mono 300 in part 1 versus part 2 were provided in Table 92. Both combination doses showed a statistically significant improvement in PFS compared to its randomized monotherapy after adjusting for baseline covariates. The HR comparing Combo 300 to Mono 300 in part 2, 0.56 (95% CI: 0.41, 0.77), is numerically lower than the HR comparing Combo 450 to Mono 300 in part 1, 0.75 (95% CI: 0.56, 0.99).
L1575: Time to PFS was also compared between two encorafenib monotherapies in part 1 and part 2 to evaluate whether efficacy results between Part 1 and Part 2 are comparable. Based on cox regression after adjusting for significant baseline covariates, patients receiving Mono 300 in Part 2 had a trend of higher risk of progression or death compared to patients receiving Mono 300 in Part 1 (HR: 1.29, 95% CI: 0.92, 1.82).

L1583: The overall survival (OS) data is still premature to conduct formal analysis. Per study protocol, the interim analysis will be conducted when approximately 232 OS events have occurred in the Combo 450 and vemurafenib arms combined. An independent OS analysis was conducted by the reviewer based on the data pertaining to dates of deaths and survival follow-up status as of 09 November 2016. There is a total of 81 events out of 192 patients treated with Combo 450, 86 events out of 258 patients treated with Combo 300 and 98 events out of 191 patients treated with vemurafenib based on the data in applicant's original submission. In addition, there are 94 events out of 194 patients and 29 events out of 86 patients in the Mono 300 in Part 1 and 2, respectively.
L1584: The Kaplan-Meier (KM) curve of OS for monotherapy and combination therapy in each part of study CMEK162B2301 were presented in Figure 31. The median time to OS is 31.2 months for Combo 450, and 23.1 and 19.5 months for Mono 300 in Part 1 and Part 2, respectively. The median OS is not reached for Combo 300 based on available data. The median follow-up is 10 months shorter in Combo 300 (15 months) compared to Combo 450 (25 months) The difference in time to OS between different treatment groups were characterized by a Cox proportional-hazards (CPH) model. Stepwise selection was conducted to screen significant covariates to be included in the final model. The model selection and the covariates included in the full model were same as those in the cox analysis for PFS. The HRs obtained from final cox regression characterizing randomized comparison of Combo 450 versus Mono 300 in Part 1, Combo 300 versus Mono 300 in Part 2 and cross-part comparison of Combo 450 versus Combo 300 and Mono 300 Part 1 versus Part 2 were provided in Table 93.
L1585: The patients treated with Combo 450 had a statistically significantly lower risk of death compared to its randomized monotherapy after adjusting for baseline covariates, and the HR
L1586: ## Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 210496
## BRAFTOVI™ (encorafenib)
L1587: comparing Combo 450 to Mono 300 in Part 1 is numerically lower than the HR comparing Combo 300 to Mono 300 in Part 2 (0.658 (95% CI: 0.486, 0.892) vs. 0.824 (0.537, 1.26)). The cross-part comparison between two combination therapies showed that patients treated with Combo 450 had a trend of lower risk of death compared to Combo 300 with HR estimated to be 0.729 (95% CI: 0.519, 1.02). In addition, no strong difference in risks of death were found between patients receiving Mono 300 in Part 1 and Part 2 (HR: 0.973, 95% CI: 0.616, 1.54, although the median OS in part 2 is 3.6 months shorter). The results from dose response analyses for OS are preliminary and should be interpreted with caution as it is important to note that both arms are immature, especially considering patients treated in Part 2 are followed approximately 10 months shorter than the patients in Part 1.

L1593: ## 19.2.2.2 Dose-Response Analyses for Safety
L1594: The safety profile between Combo 450 and Combo 300 were also compared. Time to general safety event or time to safety event of special interest were compared between two combo doses using cox proportional analysis. The HR of time to evaluated safety event of combination therapy (Combo 450 or Combo 300) relative to its randomized monotherapy in each part was
L1595: estimated and compared. A cross-part comparison between two combo doses was also conducted using cox proportional hazard model.
L1596: For general safety profiles, time to occurrence of first AE leading to drug discontinuation, first safety event of special interest of encorafenib, first dose adjustment/reduction, first grade 3/4 AE and first SAE were compared between treatment groups in study CMEK162B2301. The Kaplan-Meier (KM) curve of time to general safety events of interest in each part of study CMEK162B2301 were presented in Figure 32. The combination therapy appears to have a better safety profile than monotherapy and vemurafenib arm. In general median time to safety event also appears to be longer in Combo 300 than Combo 450.
L1597: The HRs obtained from final cox regression comparing time to general safety events of interest of Combo 450 to Mono 300 in Part 1, Combo 300 to Mono 300 in Part 2 and Combo 450 to Combo 300 were provided in Table 94. It was also visualized in the forest plot shown in Figure 8. The HR comparing Combo 300 to Mono 300 in Part 2 is numerically lower than the HR comparing Combo 450 to Mono 300 in Part 1 for time to first safety event of special interest of encorafenib, first dose adjustment/reduction, first grade 3/4 AE and first SAE. Cross-part comparison shows patients in Combo 450 had a statistically significantly higher risk of having special interest of encorafenib and grade 3/4 AE compared to Combo 300 with HR estimated to be 1.47 (95% CI: 1.14, 1.91) and 1.46 (95% CI: 1.12, 1.9), respectively. Patients in Combo 450 also tend to have higher risks of experiencing SAE and dose adjustment/reduction compared to Combo 300, but the difference is not statistically significant. For the time to first AE leading to discontinuation, there appears to be no difference between Combo 450 and Combo 300.

L1606: Time to event analysis were also conducted using Cox regression analysis with same methodology for safety events of special interest which include: AST &gt; 5x ULN or ALT &gt; 5x ULN, Grade 2 or above Hemorrhage, Grade 2 or above Myopathy, Grade 2 or above Hand-Foot Syndrome, Grade 2 or above Rash, Grade 1 or above Retinopathy Excluding RVO, Grade 2 or above Skin Infections, definitive deterioration in LVEF, first QTcF increase from baseline&gt;30 ms and first new QTcF&gt;450 ms. The HRs obtained from final cox regression comparing time to special safety events of interest of Combo 450 to Mono 300 in Part 1, Combo 300 to Mono 300 in Part 2 and Combo 450 to Combo 300 were visualized in Figure 33. Based on the Cox regression analysis, patients in the combination therapy had significantly lower risks of developing grade 2+ myopathy, grade 2+ hand-foot syndrome, grade 2+ rash compared to patients in the monotherapy. On the other hand, they have significantly higher risks of experiencing definite deterioration in LVEF and grade 1+ retinopathy excluding RVO compared to patients in the monotherapy. No difference in time to occurrence was detected between Combo 450 and Combo 300 in the cross-part comparison except for grade 1+ retinopathy excluding RVO. Patients treated with Combo 450 had significantly higher risks of developing grade 1+ retinopathy excluding RVO compared to patients in the Combo 300 group. The risks of developing liver toxicity or grade 2+ hemorrhage also appear to be higher in patients treated with combination therapy compared to monotherapy and in patients treated with Combo 450 compared to Combo 300, but the difference is not statistically significant. For the time to first QTcF increase from baseline&gt;30 ms and first new QTcF&gt;450 ms, there appears to be no difference between Combo 450 and Combo 300.
L1607: **Figure 33: The HRs Comparing Combination Therapy to the Randomized Monotherapy and HR Comparing Combo 450 to Combo 300 after Adjusting for Baseline Covariates for Evaluated Special Safety Event of Interest in Study CMEK162B2301.**
L1608: *ASTALT: Time to AST &gt; 5x ULN or ALT &gt; 5x ULN; FHEMORR: Time to First Grade 2 or above Hemorrhage; FMYOPA: Time to First Grade 2 or above Myopathy; FPALMAR: Time to First Grade 2 or above Hand-Foot Syndrome; FRASH: Time to First Grade 2 or above Rash; FRERVO: Time to First Grade 1 or above Retinopathy Excluding RVO; FSKINFE: Time to First Grade 2 or above Skin Infections; LVEF: Time to Definitive Deterioration in LVEF; QTCF1: Time to First QTcF Increase from baseline&gt;30 ms; QTCF3: Time to First new QTcF&gt;450 ms
L1609: Source: Reviewer's analysis based on dataset "adttes.xpt"
L1610: ## 19.2.3. Exposure Response Analyses
L1611: The population of ER analysis between encorafenib exposure and efficacy and safety endpoints includes a total of 449 patients among whom 192 patients were treated with Combo 450 in part 1 and 257 patients were treated with Combo 300 in Part 2. Model-predicted steady-state exposure metrics (SS Cavg, SS Ctrough and SS AUC) at day 15 were selected for the primary analyses of E-R. Pre-specified baseline covariates tested in dose-response analyses listed in section 19.4.2.1 were first added to the full models to adjust for potential confounding effects. The covariates with p-value larger than 0.05 were removed from the full model after backward elimination.
L1612: ## 19.2.3.1 Encorafenib Exposure and Efficacy
L1613: The relationship between encorafenib exposure and objective response rate was characterized using logistic regression. The parameter estimates of the final logistic regression relating predicted steady state exposure metrics as continuous variable to ORR was provided in Table 95. The crude rates of objective response rates (ORRs) were also compared among patients in different exposure quartiles (Table 96). Overall, no associations were found between encorafenib exposure and ORR in 449 patients in study CMEK162B2301. Crude rates of ORR are found comparable between patients with low encorafenib exposure and patients with high encorafenib exposure.

L1636: ## 19.2.3.2 Encorafenib Exposure and Safety
L1637: The relationship between encorafenib exposure and time to safety endpoints was characterized using cox proportional hazard models. The general safety endpoints and safety events of special interest were same as those evaluated in the dose-response analyses. The final parameter estimates for relationship between encorafenib AUC as continuous variable and time to safety endpoints were provided in Table 99 for general safety endpoints and in Table 100 for safety events of special interest. The ER analyses also evaluated the effect of encorafenib exposure quartiles on safety risks. Table 101 contains the rates of evaluated safety events in patients with encorafenib exposure below median and above median, as well as the HRs in safety risks comparing patients with low exposure to patients with high exposure.
L1638: ## Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 210496
## BRAFTOVI™ (encorafenib)
L1639: Overall, no statistically significant relationship was found between encorafenib AUC as continuous variable and time to evaluated safety events. The HRs in safety risks comparing patients with low encorafenib exposure to patients with high encorafenib exposure in the combination therapy were consistent with dose response analyses. Based on the cox regression analysis, patients with low encorafenib exposure had significantly lower risks of having grade 3/4 AE, safety event of special interest for encorafenib and grade 1+ retinopathy compared to patients with high encorafenib exposure. The risks of developing other safety events such as AE leading to discontinuation, liver toxicity, grade 2+ hemorrhage, grade 2+ rash and definitive deterioration in LVEF also appear to be lower in patients with low encorafenib exposure compared to patients with high encorafenib exposure, but their differences are not statistically significant.

L1673: ## 19.2.3.4 Binimetinib Exposure and Safety
L1674: The relationship between binimetinib exposure and time to safety endpoints was characterized using cox proportional hazard models. The general safety endpoints and safety events of special interest were same as those evaluated in the dose-response analyses. The final parameter estimates for relationship between binimetinib AUC as continuous variable and time to safety endpoints were provided in Table 106 for general safety endpoints and in Table 107 for safety events of special interest. In general, no statistically significant relationship was found between binimetinib AUC as continuous variable and time to evaluated safety events except for time to first dose adjustment/reduction. Patients with higher binimetinib exposure were more likely to experience dose adjustment/reduction based on the analysis.
L1675: ## Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 210496
## BRAFTOVI™ (encorafenib)
L1676: The ER analyses also evaluated the effect of binimetinib exposure quartile on safety risks. Table 108 contains the rates of evaluated safety events in patients with binimetinib exposure below median and above median, as well as the HRs in safety risks comparing patients with low exposure to patients with high exposure. Based on the cox regression analysis, patients with low binimetinib exposure had significantly lower risks of experiencing dose adjustment/reduction compared to patients with high binimetinib exposure. On the other hand, they have significantly higher risks of experiencing serious adverse events and grade 2+ hand-foot syndrome compared to patients with high binimetinib exposure.
L1677: The risks of developing other safety events such as grade 2+ hemorrhage, grade 2+ rash, grade 2+ skin infections and QTcF&gt; 450ms appear to be higher in patients with low binimetinib exposure, and the risks of developing liver toxicity and grade 1+ retinopathy excluding RVO appear to be higher in patients with high exposure, but their differences are not statistically significant.

L1689: In summary, DR relationships for efficacy suggest that encorafenib 450mg and 300mg QD in combination therapy have a comparable ORR and PFS. However, OS appears to be better in encorafenib dosing regimen 450mg QD compared to 300mg QD in combination with
L1690: binimetinib based on preliminary analyses. Therefore, there is insufficient evidence that Combo 300 is as effective as Combo 450. Cross-part comparison of safety profile between Combo 300 and Combo 450 did not indicate a clinically significant safety concern for Combo 450 relative to Combo 300 which would outweigh the potential for improved efficacy in higher dose. The ER relationships were consistent with DR relationships, and they both support the use of the proposed encorafenib dose of 450 mg QD with dose modification in the event of adverse reactions to the lowest dose of 200 mg.
L1691: ## 19.2.4. **Physiological-based Pharmacokinetic Modeling Review**
L1692: ## 19.2.4.1. Objectives
L1693: To evaluate the adequacy of applicant's physiologically-based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) model to predict the steady state drug-drug interaction (DDI) between posaconazole or itraconazole (two strong CYP3A inhibitors) and encorafenib (a CYP3A substrate) at the therapeutic dose levels in cancer patients.
L1694: ## 19.2.4.2. Background
L1695: Encorafenib is a selective BRAF kinase inhibitor that is primarily eliminated by CYP3A4 metabolism. In vitro data suggested that encorafenib is both a time-dependent inhibitor and an inducer of CYP3A4. In Study CMEK162X2110, the geometric mean ratios on encorafenib Cmax and AUC between Day 15 and Day 1 values were 0.63 and 0.45, respectively, suggesting a net effect of auto-induction on CYP3A4 across the dose range of 50 and 600 mg QD. In Study ARRAY-818-105, co-administration of strong (posaconazole) and moderate (diltiazem) CYP3A4 inhibitors with a single dose of 50 mg encorafenib resulted in 3- and 2-fold increase in AUC and 1.7 and 1.5-fold increase in Cmax. Even though encorafenib is a P-gp substrate, the contribution of P-gp inhibition by posaconazole and diltiazem was difficult to assess in Study ARRAY-818-105. The Tmax values in different treatment arms in the study were similar, suggesting that the observed DDIs were not due to differences in absorption rates, but due to inhibition of CYP3A4 metabolism. Study ARRAY-818-102 indicated a lack of food effect on the extent of exposure to encorafenib following a single oral 100 mg dose. The applicant developed an encorafenib PBPK model to describe the PK profiles of encorafenib based on in silico, in vitro, and clinical PK data.
L1696: On November 3 and November 22, 2017, two information requests (IRs) were issued, requesting a revision of the submitted PBPK model to better describe the observed encorafenib exposure after repeated doses of encorafenib between 200 and 550 mg dose level, to conduct the DDI simulations with posaconazole and itraconazole in fed condition, and to improve model performance in predicting the food effect on encorafenib.
L1697: This review evaluates the adequacy of the applicant's final PBPK model to predict the steady state DDI effect of CYP3A inhibitors on encorafenib at 450 mg QD levels in cancer patients.
L1698: ## 19.2.4.3. Method
L1699: ## **Summary of Clinical Studies Used in Encorafenib Model Development and Verification**
L1700: Study	N*	Relevant Study Features
CLGX818A2101	4	ADME study: Single oral dose of 100 mg [^{14}\text{C}]encorafenib given as a micro-emulsion
ARRAY-162-105	15	DDI study: Single dose of 300 mg encorafenib alone, and single dose of 100 mg encorafenib alone or in combination with 20 mg rabeprazole. PK data from single agent encorafenib treatments were used.
CLGX818X2101	54	Phase 1, dose-escalation study in patients: Oral encorafenib administered as micro-emulsion solution or as capsules. PK data for capsules were used.
ARRAY-818-102	31	Food effect: Single dose of 100 mg encorafenib under fasted and fed conditions
ARRAY-818-105	32	DDI study: Single dose of 50 mg encorafenib alone or in combination with posaconazole or diltiazem. Only encorafenib PK data were collected in the study.
L1701: * N refers to the number of patients in the study with encorafenib PK data; for Study CLGX818X2101 it refers to patients with PK data in the dose escalation phase although there was also PK data in the dose expansion phase.
L1702: Source: adapted from cp17-002-a3-report-body.pdf, Table 2
L1703: **Reviewer's Comment:** In model verification, the sponsor compared the PBPK predicted steady state exposure to the population PK (popPK) predicted steady state Cmax and AUC for the 300 mg QD and 450 mg QD treatments in Study CMEK162B2301. Comparison to popPK model prediction is not considered a valid qualification especially when the encorafenib PBPK model was developed and refined based on popPK model estimates. Simulation results for Study CMEK162B2301 will not be discussed in this review.
L1704: ## **Software**
L1705: GastroPlus™ (Version 9.0) was used to develop the PBPK model. The ADMET Predictor™ module was used to obtain in silico estimates of physicochemical and biopharmaceutical properties from structure if experimentally determined values were not available, or to provide an objective alternative to experimental data. The PBPKPlus™ module was used to simulate the pharmacokinetic distribution and systemic clearance of encorafenib. The Parameter Sensitivity Analysis mode was used to assess the importance of a variety of parameters in predicting absorption and pharmacokinetics. The DDI module was used to predict the effect of encorafenib auto induction of CYP3A4 on its own PK and to predict the effect of CYP3A inhibitors on encorafenib.
L1706: ## **Encorafenib Model Development**
L1707: The Advanced Compartmental Absorption and Transit (ACAT™) model was used to describe the drug disposition in each region of the intestinal tract. The default Johnson model was used to model drug dissolution. Intestinal absorption was modeled as passive diffusion. The human jejunal P_{\text{eff}} was optimized to match clinical observation. The absorption rate coefficients in individual intestinal compartments are calculated from P_{\text{eff}} and absorption scale factors (ASFs). The intestinal first-pass metabolism of encorafenib by CYP2C19 and CYP3A4 enzymes was modeled using Michaelis-Menten kinetics with the same K_m and V_{\text{max}} values for liver metabolism (described below).
L1708: A PBPK model was developed with all perfusion-limited tissues. Human organ weights, volumes, and blood perfusion rates specific to subjects in each study (gender, age, and body
L1709: weight) were generated by GastroPlus internal Population Estimates for Age-Related (PEAR) Physiology™ Module. Drug tissue:plasma partition coefficients (Kps) were estimated using the Lukacova method using drug-specific and system-specific parameters. Kps were further adjusted by using a lower logP value to better match the shape of PK profiles in Study ARRAY-818-102. An adjusted Fup, 12.76%, instead of the in-vitro reported value 13.9%, was used in the PBPK model to account for possible drug binding to plasma lipids as may occur with more lipophilic compounds.
L1710: Systemic clearance of encorafenib was limited to CYP2C19 and CYP3A4 metabolism using Michaelis-Menten kinetics. In the initial model, Km were set to in-vitro measurements and Vmax values were fitted to in vivo plasma concentration-time profiles for 100 mg doses in healthy subjects and 50-700 mg doses in cancer patients in the fasted state. In model refinement, sponsor conducted sensitivity analysis on CYP3A4 Km to improve model prediction of food effect. For each Km value, Vmax was fitted to match observed fasted AUC for a single 100 mg dose from the ARRAY-818-102 study, and subsequently used to simulate exposure for a single 100 mg dose in fed state. A CYP3A4 Km, 213.9 µg/mL, 10-fold higher than reported in-vitro Km was used in the final PBPK model. Vmax values in patient population were lower to 88.6% of those in healthy subjects based on differences between the two populations from the encorafenib population PK model.
L1711: To account for changes in encorafenib metabolism after multiple doses, the effect of encorafenib on CYP3A4 activity was modeled as a concentration-independent induction with parameters EC50 and Emax fitted to clinical observations in Studies CLGX818X2101 and ARRAY-818-102. Time-dependent inhibition, as evident in in vitro assays, was not included in the final PBPK model.
L1712: Table 112 in Appendix provides a summary of drug-specific parameters in the encorafenib model.
L1713: ## ***Reviewer's comment:***
L1714: - *Sponsor's encorafenib PBPK model was developed based on the following assumptions: 1) Tissue distribution of encorafenib was limited by blood flow to tissues; 2) Encorafenib permeability is through the passive route only; and 3) the effect of P-gp on absorption was negligible. These assumptions were supported by the high logP and hence high permeability.*
- *Sponsor's encorafenib PBPK model was developed based on the assumption that 1) the metabolism of encorafenib was limited to CYP3A4 and CYP2C19; and 2) renal elimination of encorafenib was minimal. These assumptions were supported by data from Study CLGX818A2101.*
- *It should be noted that the effect of encorafenib as a CYP3A4 modulator has not been qualified with any probe drug for CYP3A substrate. The prediction of DDI potential between encorafenib and a modulator drug which is also a CYP3A substrate should be interpreted with caution.*
L1715: ## Encorafenib Model Verification
L1716: When simulating PK data from clinical studies for model verification, the mean age of subjects, mean weight of subjects, gender of the majority of the study population, disease state (healthy or cancer patient), and the fed or fasted state specified in clinical protocol were matched in the PBPK model. Male PBPK model was used for studies where the group of subjects included an equal number of males and females. The IR: Capsule formulation was selected in all simulations. Stomach transit time was set to 0.1 hours in fasted state, 1 hour in fed, and 0.5 hour in non-fasted state. Unless specified, all simulations were conducted with encorafenib auto-induction of CYP3A4 in the DDI module. Verification simulations included:
L1717: - Prediction of encorafenib PK in healthy subjects after single doses of 50, 100, or 300 mg encorafenib as micro-emulsion or capsule. (Studies CLGX818A2101, ARRAY-818-105, and ARRAY-162-105)
- Prediction of encorafenib PK in cancer patients receiving 50 to 550 QD doses in Study CLGX818X2101.
- Prediction of food effect in healthy subjects receiving a single dose of 100 mg encorafenib in the fasted and fed status (Study ARRAY-818-102).
- Prediction of DDI with posaconazole in healthy subjects receiving a single dose of 50 mg encorafenib (Study ARRAY-818-105). The DDI simulation was conducted such that 400 mg posaconazole was given twice daily from Day 1-9 in the fed condition excepted for the first dose on Day 7, when it was administered with a single dose of 50 mg encorafenib in the fasted state. The baseline condition is a single dose of 50 me encorafenib in the fasted state.
L1718: Reviewer conducted a simulation to predict DDI with diltiazem in healthy subjects receiving a single dose of 50 mg encorafenib in the fasted condition. Reviewer's analysis was conducted with GastroPlus Version 9.5.
L1719: ## Encorafenib Model Application
L1720: The encorafenib PBPK model was used to predict the DDI effect of posaconazole (400 mg BID) and itraconazole (200 mg QD) on encorafenib at 450 mg QD and 300 mg QD dose levels in cancer patients. Itraconazole model from the standard GastroPlus database (simplified to include only parent compound and the first metabolite, OH-itraconazole) was used in the simulations. The physiology for was parameterized for a 59.3-year-old, 79.8 kg man. The simulations were conducted in the fed condition.
L1721: *Reviewer's Comment: In addition to DDI simulation with posaconazole and itraconazole, the applicant conducted a DDI simulation to evaluate the effect of rifampin (a strong CYP3A inducer) on encorafenib steady state exposure. Because the DDI module does not allow simultaneous fitting of two induction effects (one from rifampin and one from encorafenib in this case), the simulation only included the effects of rifampicin on CYP3A4 expression. The simulation was based on the assumptions that induction in the gut was at a maximum from rifampin and the induction in the liver was driven by rifampicin. However, there were no*
L1722: *adequate clinical data to verify these assumptions. The PBPK analysis was not adequate to predict DDI potential between encorafenib and rifampin.*
L1723: ## **Posaconazole and Diltiazem Models**
L1724: Sponsor developed a posaconazole and a diltiazem PBPK model using clinical data from literature. The posaconazole-midazolam DDI simulation settings and the diltiazem-midazolam DDI simulation settings were not submitted. Based on the summary of model predictions, the posaconazole and diltiazem PBPK models appear adequate in terms of their capacity to predict posaconazole / diltiazem PK as a single agent and to predict their effect as a strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitor on midazolam PK (Table 113 and Table 114 in Appendix).
L1725: ## 19.2.4.4. Result
L1726: ## **Can PBPK model capture encorafenib exposure under different dosing scenario as a single agent?**
L1727: Yes. Sponsor's PBPK model were developed, refined, and verified based on results of: (1) single-dose PK data on encorafenib in healthy subjects from Studies CLGX818A2101, ARRAY-818-105, and ARRAY-162-105 (Figure 38); (2) single- and multiple-dose PK data on encorafenib in cancer patients in Study CLGX818X2101 (Figure 39); and (3) food effect on a single-dose of 100 mg encorafenib in healthy subjects in Study ARRAY-818-102 (Figure 40). The prediction errors in Cmax and AUC, across all doses in healthy volunteers and in cancer patients (on both days), are within 2-fold in all the simulations, except for the first day treatment with 50 mg encorafenib in Study CLGX818X2101. The Cmax and AUC after a single dose of 50 mg encorafenib in cancer patients were under-predicted with a predicted / simulated AUC ratio (AUCR) of 0.44 and Cmax ratio (CmaxR) of 0.46. Only one subject provided PK data in the 50 mg treatment group in Study CLGX818X2101. Consistent with minimal food effect observed in Study ARRAY-818-102, the final model predicted a 4% decrease in AUC_{inf} when a single dose of 100 mg encorafenib was administered in the fed state than in a fasted state. Model performance in capturing encorafenib exposure when encorafenib was administered alone is acceptable.
L1728: It is noted that the simulated Day 15/Day 1 AUC ratios (between 0.66-0.71) are higher than that based on observation (approximately 0.5). There does not appear to be a correlation between dose and the Day 15/Day 1 AUC ratio in the range of 50-550 mg, based on either simulation or observation.
L1729: **Figure 38.** Observed (points) and PBPK-predicted (lines) plasma-time profiles of encorafenib in healthy subjects taking a single dose of encorafenib.
L1730: Source: PBPK report (cp17-002-a3-report-body.pdf), Figure 6-9
L1731: **Figure 39.** Observed (points) and PBPK-predicted (lines) plasma-time profiles of encorafenib in cancer patients receiving QD doses of encorafenib as capsule.
L1732: Source: PBPK report (cp17-002-a3-report-body.pdf), Figure 10-17
L1733: **Figure 40.** Observed (points) and PBPK-predicted (lines) plasma-time profiles of encorafenib in healthy subjects taking a single dose of 100 mg encorafenib the fasted (left) and fed (right) status.
L1734: Source: PBPK report (cp17-002-a3-report-body.pdf), Figure 2 and 3
L1735: ## Can PBPK model capture encorafenib exposure under the effect of posaconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor?
L1736: Yes. Sponsor's encorafenib PBPK model was verified based on the results of single-dose PK data (Figure 41) and fold change (Table 109) in encorafenib exposure in Study ARRAY-818-105 with posaconazole.
L1737: **Figure 41.** Observed (points) and PBPK-predicted (lines) plasma-time profiles of encorafenib in healthy subjects taking a single dose of 50 mg encorafenib with posaconazole.
L1738: Source: PBPK report (cp17-002-a3-report-body.pdf), Figure 19

L1744: ## **Can PBPK model capture encorafenib exposure under the effect of diltiazem, a moderate CYP3A inhibitor?**
L1745: No. The encorafenib-diltiazem PBPK model significantly under-estimated the DDI effect by diltiazem on encorafenib exposure after a single dose of encorafenib (Table 110).
L1746: Removing encorafenib auto-induction from the encorafenib-diltiazem DDI simulation would increase encorafenib AUC by 123% (without vs with auto induction AUCR=2.23) and increase diltiazem AUC by 26% (AUCR=1.26). Removing encorafenib auto-induction from the simulation of encorafenib as a single agent would increase the predicted (steady state) AUC by approximately 40% (AUCR=1.41) at the same dose level. The impact of auto-induction is significantly higher in the DDI simulations, suggesting that a 26% increase in diltiazem exposure is clinically relevant in terms of inhibitory effect on CYP3A4 and on encorafenib PK.

Ratio [combination/single-agent]	1.45	1.08	0.74	1.87	1.09	0.58
L1749: Source: Reviewer's analysis using sponsor's diltiazem and encorafenib PBPK models.
L1750: ## **Can PBPK model predict encorafenib exposure change when the drug is co-administered with strong CYP3A inhibitors, posaconazole and itraconazole, at the 300 mg QD or 450 mg QD dose levels?**
L1751: PBPK model can be used to predict posaconazole effect on 450 mg QD doses of encorafenib. The encorafenib PBPK model was verified using clinical DDI study with posaconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor. While encorafenib parameters on CYP3A metabolism, inhibition, and induction were fitted to clinical observations, PK data from the encorafenib-posaconazole treatment in the DDI study was not used in encorafenib model building. The model predicted a 2.8-fold and 3.6-fold increase in single day and steady state AUC, when 450 mg QD encorafenib is co-administered with 400 mg BID posaconazole (Table 111).
L1752: The predicted DDI effect by itraconazole on encorafenib is associated with uncertainty. The encorafenib PBPK model has not been verified for its induction/inhibitory effect on a probe drug for CYP3A4 substrate (such as midazolam). Itraconazole is primarily eliminated by CYP3A4. The effect of encorafenib auto-induction and competitive inhibition on itraconazole exposure and consequently, itraconazole effect on encorafenib exposure may not be predicted with confidence.
L1753: **Table 111. Predicted effect of CYP3A4 inhibitors on encorafenib exposures in cancer patients**
L1754: Day 1	Steady State
CmaxR	AUCR	CmaxR	AUCR
Posaconazole (400 mg BID)	2.60	2.77	3.27	3.60
Itraconazole (200 mg QD)	2.60	2.79	3.19	3.47
L1755: ## 19.2.4.5. Conclusion
L1756: The applicant's PBPK model for encorafenib can be used to predict encorafenib PK after single- and multiple- doses of encorafenib as a single agent. The model can be used for the prediction of DDI effect between posaconazole (a strong CYP3A inhibitor) and encorafenib at the therapeutic dose level. Encorafenib AUC at 450 mg QD level is predicted to increase by 2.8-fold after a single dose and 3.6-fold at steady state, with co-administration with posaconazole at 400 mg BID.
L1757: ## 19.2.4.6. Information Requests
L1758: November 3, 2017:
L1759: - Your encorafenib model significantly over-predicts encorafenib exposure after repeated doses of encorafenib between 200 and 550 mg dose level. Please improve your encorafenib model and justify changes made in the model parameters.
- The tablet formula was used in 'encorafenib-Healthy-baseline model.mdb' for the assessment of model performance in healthy subjects. Rationalize your choice of tablet formula and use the capsule formulation to simulate the exposure of encorafenib following repeated oral doses in the healthy subjects.
- Using the updated encorafenib model, simulate the effects of posaconazole and itraconazole on the PK of encorafenib in the fed condition. Summarize the predicted DDI effects on the PK of encorafenib (such as R_AUC and R_Cmax) after a single dose and after multiple doses of encorafenib.
L1760: November 22, 2017:
L1761: - Your encorafenib PBPK model predicts significant food effects with approximately 50% reduction in AUC after a single dose of encorafenib. This is not consistent with conclusions from the dedicated food effect study (Study ARRAY-818-102). Please improve the encorafenib PBPK model in terms of food effect and justify changes made to the model parameters.
- Using the updated encorafenib model, simulate the effects of posaconazole and itraconazole on the PK of encorafenib in the fed condition. Summarize the predicted DDI effects on the PK of encorafenib (such as R_AUC and R_Cmax) after a single dose and after multiple doses of encorafenib.

L1860: Figure 4: Anti-tumor activity of LGX818 in mice bearing A375 tumors. The graph plots Tumor Volume (mm^3) mean +/- SEM versus Time Post-Implant (Days) from Day 10 to Day 25. Other doses (0.6 mg/kg bid, 6 mg/kg bid, 60 mg/kg bid) show intermediate tumor growth, with 60 mg/kg bid showing the lowest volume (around 300 mm^3).

The right panel shows HMEX1906 tumor volume (mm^3) over 50 days (BID treatment). The 20 mg/kg BID dose shows significant regression, reaching approximately 100 mm^3 by Day 50.

L1863: Figure 7 displays three graphs showing the effects of MEK162 and LGX818 treatments on tumor volume and body weight in mice bearing HMEX1906 tumors.
The top graph, labeled "Tumor volume," plots Tumor Volume (\text{mm}^3) mean \pm SEM versus Days Post-Implant (0 to 160). The legend indicates six treatment groups: Vehicle (black squares), MEK162 3mg/kg bid (red triangles), MEK162 10mg/kg bid (blue triangles), LGX818 3mg/kg bid (green diamonds), MEK162 3mg/kg bid + LGX818 3mg/kg bid (orange circles), and MEK162 10mg/kg bid + LGX818 3mg/kg bid (purple circles). The combination therapies show significantly reduced tumor growth compared to single agents or vehicle.
The middle graph, labeled "Tumor volume (magnified view)," provides a zoomed-in view of the initial phase (Days Post-Implant 0 to 100). This graph confirms that the combination therapies (MEK162 3mg/kg bid + LGX818 3mg/kg bid and MEK162 10mg/kg bid + LGX818 3mg/kg bid) result in the lowest tumor volume, approaching zero by Day 100.
The bottom graph, labeled "Body weight," plots Change in body weight (%) mean \pm SEM versus Days Post-Implant (0 to 160). All treatment groups show minimal change in body weight, remaining close to 0% change, indicating that the observed anti-tumor effects are not associated with significant toxicity or weight loss.

Group A: BRAF and MEK naive. Proceeds to LGX818/MEK162 combination. Followed by Biopsy at PD and Genetic Assessment (Biopsy Analysis). Leads to Optional LGX818/MEK162 combination (Gen. Ass. and/or Run-in).
Group B: Any BRAF/MEK combination or single agents*. Proceeds to Run-in** LGX818/MEK162 (first scan after 3 weeks). Followed by Biopsy at PD and Genetic Assessment (Biopsy Analysis). Leads to Optional LGX818/MEK162 combination (Gen. Ass. and/or Run-in).
Group C: Columbus, LOGIC1, CMEK162X2110: LGX818/MEK162 arm. Proceeds to Optional LGX818/MEK162 combination (Gen. Ass. and/or Run-in).
Part II:
Following Part I, patients proceed to Part II, which involves treatment with one of the following combinations:
BKM120, BGJ398, INC280 or LEE011 ***
LGX818
MEK162

At Cycle 1 Day 1, the predicted peak concentration is around 600 ng/mL, occurring approximately 2 hours after the dose. At Cycle 1 Day 15, the predicted peak concentration is slightly higher, around 700 ng/mL, also occurring approximately 2 hours after the dose. Both panels show a rapid decline in concentration after the peak.

ARRAY-818-105: 50 mg capsule (Top Middle): Cp peaks around 500 ng/mL at 2 hrs. The predicted curve fits the observed data well.
Study ARRAY-162-105: 100 mg capsule (Top Right): Cp peaks around 1000 ng/mL at 2 hrs. The predicted curve fits the observed data well.
Study ARRAY-162-105: 300 mg capsule (Top Right): Cp peaks around 3000 ng/mL at 2 hrs. The predicted curve fits the observed data well.

L1896: Figure 40 shows two graphs comparing observed (points with error bars) and PBPK-predicted (lines) plasma concentration (Cp) versus time (hrs) for a single dose of 100 mg encorafenib in healthy subjects. The left graph is for the fasted state, and the right graph is for the fed state. In both graphs, Cp is measured in ng/mL and Time in hrs. The fasted profile shows a higher peak concentration (around 800 ng/mL) and faster clearance compared to the fed profile (peak around 400 ng/mL).
L1897: Figure 41 shows a graph comparing observed (points with error bars) and PBPK-predicted (lines) plasma concentration (Cp) versus time (hrs) for a single dose of 50 mg encorafenib in healthy subjects co-administered with posaconazole. Cp is measured in ng/mL and Time in hrs. The profile shows a peak concentration around 1000 ng/mL occurring earlier than the single-agent profile.
L1898: Figure 42 is a line graph illustrating the Mean FACT-M Overall Score by Treatment Arm and Visit in Part 1. The Y-axis represents the Mean FACT-M Overall Score by Visit, ranging from 50 to 55. The X-axis represents the Visit, including Baseline, Cycle 3 Day 1, Cycle 5 Day 1, Cycle 7 Day 1, Cycle 9 Day 1, Cycle 11 Day 1, Cycle 13 Day 1, Cycle 15 Day 1, Cycle 17 Day 1, Cycle 19 Day 1, Cycle 21 Day 1, Cycle 23 Day 1, and Cycle 25 Day 1.
The graph displays three treatment arms:
Vemurafenib (Red line)
Encorafenib (Part 1) (Green line)
Combo 450 (Blue line)
At Baseline, the Combo 450 arm shows the highest score (approx. 52.5), followed by Vemurafenib (approx. 52.0), and Encorafenib (Part 1) (approx. 53.0). The Combo 450 arm generally maintains the highest score throughout the treatment period, peaking around Cycle 23 Day 1 (approx. 55.5). The Vemurafenib arm shows significant fluctuations, dropping sharply after Cycle 3 Day 1 and peaking around Cycle 19 Day 1 (approx. 54.0). The Encorafenib (Part 1) arm shows a steady increase in score, reaching its peak around Cycle 25 Day 1 (approx. 54.0).

L1901: Line graph showing the Mean Change from Baseline FACT-M Overall Score by Visit for three treatment arms: Vemurafenib (red line), Encorafenib (Part 1) (green line), and Combo 450 (blue line). The Combo 450 arm generally shows the highest mean change (improvement) from baseline, peaking around 1.0 at Cycle 7 Day 1. The Vemurafenib arm shows significant fluctuations, with a peak around 0.8 at Cycle 17 Day 1. The Encorafenib (Part 1) arm generally shows the lowest mean change (worse performance), often below -1.0, with a trough around -3.0 at Cycle 11 Day 1.
